The plots clearly show that the deepshared CovNet is able to capture a much richer structure than the separable model. While the leading eigenfunction of the CovNet model accounts for
42.74\%
of the variability explained, the same for the separable model is only
0.0004\%
. In fact, to explain
42.74\%
variability, we would need
45538
components in the separable model. Moreover, the fitted CovNet is able to extract the natural symmetry in the brain during resting state
(
Raemaekers et al. 2018
)
, while no such constraints were imposed a priori.

In Figure
10
, we show the second and the third leading eigenfunctions of the fitted CovNet which, together with the leading eigenfunction, account for
88.64\%
of the variability. In contrast, for the separable model, we would require
84578
components to explain
88.64\%
variability. The plots clearly exhibit the ability of the deepshared CovNet to identify complex structures from the data.

6
Asymptotic theory

We conclude the article by developing asymptotic theory for our estimators. In particular, we prove that the CovNet estimators are consistent, and derive their rates of convergence. We will consider two different setups – (a) when the fields are fully observed and (b) when the fields are observed on a grid with possible noise contamination.

6.1
The case of fully observed fields

Here, our data consist of i.i.d. random fields
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}
distributed as
\mathcal{X}
. For convenience, we start by assuming that the random field
\mathcal{X}
is bounded, i.e., there exists
\beta_{N}>0
such that
\|\mathcal{X}\|^{2}\leq\beta_{N}
almost surely. This type of boundedness assumption is quite common in the theoretical analysis of neural networks
(
Györfi et al. 2002
;
Schmidt-Hieber 2020
, e.g.,)
. Note that the bound is allowed to grow with
N
, so this can also be seen as a growing truncation level. We will eventually remove this boundedness condition.

We also need to impose some condition on the approximating class, for which we restrict the eigenstructure of the matrix
\Lambda=((\lambda_{r,s}))
in (
3.1
). Specifically, for a constant
\lambda_{N}>0
, we enforce that
\Lambda\preceq\lambda_{N}\mathrm{I}_{R}
. Note that for any non-negative definite matrix
\Lambda
, we always have
\Lambda\preceq\lambda_{\max}\mathrm{I}_{R}
, where
\lambda_{\max}
is the largest eigenvalue of
\Lambda
. Thus, our assumption imposes a restriction on the largest eigenvalue of the matrix
\Lambda
in (
3.1
). We write
\mathscr{F}_{R,\lambda_{N}}
to denote the restricted class of kernels corresponding to the generic class
\mathscr{F}_{R}
. In particular,

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{\rm sh}_{R,\lambda_{N}}

\displaystyle=\big\{c_{\rm sh}\text{ of the form \eqref{eq:shallow_covnet_kernel}}:\mathrm{0}\preceq\Lambda=((\lambda_{r,s}))\preceq\lambda_{N}{\rm I}_{R},\mathbf{w}_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},b_{r}\in\mathbb{R}\big\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{\rm d}_{R,L,\lambda_{N}}

\displaystyle=\big\{c_{\rm d}\text{ of the form \eqref{eq:deep_covnet_kernel}}:\mathrm{0}\preceq\Lambda:=((\lambda_{r,s}))\preceq\lambda_{N}\,\mathrm{I}_{R},g_{1},\ldots,g_{R}\in\mathcal{D}_{L,R}\big\}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,\lambda_{N}}

\displaystyle=\big\{c_{\rm ds}\text{ of the form \eqref{eq:deepshared_covnet_kernel}}:\mathrm{0}\preceq\Lambda:=((\lambda_{r,s}))\preceq\lambda_{N}\mathrm{I}_{R},

\displaystyle\kern 129.16626ptg_{1},\ldots,g_{R}\text{ of the form \eqref{eq:deepshared_neural_network} with }p_{1}=\cdots=p_{L}=R\big\},

(6.1)

denote the restricted shallow, deep, and deepshared CovNet classes. In the above, we write
\mathcal{D}_{L,R}
to denote the deep neural network class
\mathcal{D}_{L,\mathbf{p}}
when
p_{1}=\cdots=p_{L}=R
. We also denote the class of operators corresponding to the class of kernels
\mathscr{F}_{R,\lambda_{N}}
by
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R,\lambda_{N}}
. Recall that our estimators are defined as

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm sh}_{R,N}\in\operatornamewithlimits{arg\,min}_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm sh}_{R,\lambda_{N}}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{N}-\mathcal{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2},~\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm d}_{R,L,N}\in\operatornamewithlimits{arg\,min}_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm d}_{R,L,\lambda_{N}}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{N}-\mathcal{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}~\text{ and }~\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,N}\in\operatornamewithlimits{arg\,min}_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,\lambda_{N}}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{N}-\mathcal{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}.

(6.2)

In the following, we start by proving two different kinds of results. First, we prove consistency of the estimators under appropriate conditions, and then derive their rates of convergence.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}\overset{{\rm i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{X}
, where
\mathcal{X}
takes values in
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
, and
\mathcal{Q}
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Also assume that
\|\mathcal{X}\|^{2}\leq\beta_{N}
almost surely,
\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{X})=0
and
\mathrm{Cov}(\mathcal{X})=\mathcal{C}
. Let
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{R,N}^{\rm sh}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{R,L,N}^{\rm d}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{R,L,N}^{\rm ds}
be the shallow, the deep, and the deepshared CovNet estimators given by (
6.2
). Suppose that
R\to\infty,\lambda_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
, and define
\Delta_{N}=\max\{\beta_{N},|\mathcal{Q}|R\lambda_{N}\}
.

(A)

If
dR^{2}\Delta_{N}^{4}\log(\Delta_{N})/N\to 0
as
N\to\infty
, then the shallow CovNet estimator is weakly consistent for
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm sh}_{R,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}\overset{P}{\to}0
. Additionally, if
\Delta_{N}^{4}/N^{1-\delta}\to 0
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, then the estimator is strongly consistent for
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm sh}_{R,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
as
N\to\infty
.

(B)

Let
R>d
. If
L^{4}R^{8}\Delta_{N}^{4}\log^{2}(L\Delta_{N})/N\to 0
as
N\to\infty
, then the deep CovNet estimator is weakly consistent for
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm d}_{R,L,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}\overset{P}{\to}0
. Additionally, if
L^{4}R^{8}\Delta_{N}^{4}\log^{2}(L\Delta_{N})/N^{1-\delta}\to 0
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, then the estimator is strongly consistent for
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
as
N\to\infty
.

(C)

Let
R>d
. If
L^{4}R^{6}\Delta_{N}^{4}\log^{2}(L\Delta_{N})/N\to 0
, then the deepshared CovNet estimator is weakly consistent for
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}\overset{P}{\to}0
. Additionally, if
L^{4}R^{6}\Delta_{N}^{4}\log^{2}(L\Delta_{N})/N^{1-\delta}\to 0
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, then the estimator is strongly consistent for
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
as
N\to\infty
.

The proof of the theorem involves
bias-variance-type decompositions
for the estimation error
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}
. To control the bias term, we need the universal approximation property (Theorems
1
and
2
), but now with the additional restriction on the classes (
6.1
). This is ensured by assuming that
R
,
L
and
\lambda_{N}
go to infinity as
N
diverges (see Remark
11
). On the other hand,
dR^{2}\Delta_{N}^{4}\log(\Delta_{N})/N
,
L^{4}R^{8}\Delta_{N}^{4}\log^{2}(L\Delta_{N})/N
, and
L^{4}R^{6}\Delta_{N}^{4}\log^{2}(L\Delta_{N})/N
are linked to the
variance
of the estimators. The conditions of the theorem ensures that the variance also converges to
0
with the sample size for the different estimators.

Remark 8
.

There is a small technical ambiguity in the statement of Theorem
3
. The theorem is stated for i.i.d. observations distributed as
\mathcal{X}
when
N
goes to infinity, whereas the bound on
\mathcal{X}
is also allowed to evolve with
N
. Thus, the result is to be understood for a triangular sequence of arrays where, for each
N
, the observations are bounded by a constant, which in turn is allowed to diverge keeping up with the assumption of the theorem. However, we avoid stating the theorem in this generality for ease of exposition. The special case of i.i.d. observations (i.e., when
\beta_{N}
is a constant) follows easily from the theorem.

Next, we derive the rate of convergence of the estimators.

Theorem 4
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}\overset{{\rm i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{X}
, where
\mathcal{X}
takes values in
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
, and
\mathcal{Q}
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Suppose that
\|\mathcal{X}\|^{2}\leq\beta_{N}
almost surely,
\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{X})=0
and
\mathrm{Cov}(\mathcal{X})=\mathcal{C}
. Let
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{R,N}^{\rm sh}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{R,L,N}^{\rm d}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{R,L,N}^{\rm ds}
be the shallow, the deep, and the deepshared CovNet estimators given by (
6.2
). Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big({|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm sh}_{R,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm sh}_{R,\lambda_{N}}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{dR^{2}\Delta_{N}^{4}\log(N)}{N}\bigg),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big({|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm d}_{R,L,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm d}_{R,L,\lambda_{N}}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{L^{4}R^{8}\Delta_{N}^{4}\log^{2}(N)}{N}\bigg)\text{, and }

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big({|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,\lambda_{N}}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{L^{4}R^{6}\Delta_{N}^{4}\log^{2}(N)}{N}\bigg).

Here,
\Delta_{N}=\max\{\beta_{N},|\mathcal{Q}|R\lambda_{N}\}
is as defined in Theorem
3
. In the above, for the deep and the deepshared CovNet estimators, we have assumed that
R>d
.

The theorem clearly shows the bias-variance-type decomposition for the proposed estimators. To get the exact rates of convergence, we need to quantify the bias terms, which is an approximation theoretic problem. If, for example, the bias term is zero for some finite
R,L
and
\lambda_{N}
, then the derived rate of convergence is the same as that of the empirical estimator, except for the logarithmic term. Thus, in this case, our estimator enjoys a nearly minimax rate of convergence. In general, to get the rate of convergence of the bias, we need to make further assumptions. There are two ways of doing this, either by making assumptions on the eigenstructure of the true covariance or by making assumptions on the smoothness of the underlying field
\mathcal{X}
(see Appendix
B.2
for details). For this line of derivations, the rates depend crucially on the approximation error of the constituents of the CovNet model under consideration. For instance, for the shallow CovNet, if we assume that the underlying field
\mathcal{X}
takes values in
\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{Q})
, the Sobolev space of functions of order
\alpha
on
\mathcal{Q}

(
Mhaskar 1996
, see)
, with almost surely bounded Sobolev norm (i.e.,
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{Q})}^{2}\leq\beta
a.s.), then by selecting
\lambda_{N}\asymp R
, we can bound the bias term as
\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm sh}_{R,\lambda_{N}}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}\lesssim R^{-\alpha/d}
(see (
B.11
)). So, for consistency we need
R^{10}={\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(N/(d\log(N))
, while the optimal rate is achieved for
R\asymp(N/d\log(N))^{d/(10d+\alpha)}
. This leads to the rate of convergence
\mathcal{O}\big((d\log(N)/N)^{\alpha/(10d+\alpha)}\big)
. Similarly, one can use results from
Langer 2021
;
Ohn and Kim 2019
to bound the bias of the deep and the deepshared CovNet models.

Remark 9
.

Both Theorems
3
and
4
are derived here for a global minimizer of the loss function. But, in practice, we are not guaranteed to find a global minima. Following the proof of Theorem
3
, it can be shown that the consistency results hold as long as the estimator is within
{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}_{P}(1)
of the minimizer, i.e., for estimators
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}
satisfying
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{N}-\widehat{\mathcal{C}}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}\leq\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R,\lambda_{N}}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{N}-\mathcal{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}_{P}(1)
. Similarly, from the proof of Theorem
4
, one can check that for an approximate minimizer, the rate of convergence gets inflated by the “expected minimization gap”
\mathbb{E}\Big({|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}-\widehat{\mathcal{C}}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}-\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R,\lambda_{N}}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}-\mathcal{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}\,\big|\,\mathscr{X}_{N}\Big)
, where
\mathscr{X}_{N}=\{\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}\}
denotes the observed data, and
\widetilde{C}_{N}
is distributed identically to
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{N}
but independently of
\mathscr{X}_{N}
.

Finally, we prove consistency without the boundedness condition on
\mathcal{X}
. In this case, we need to slightly modify our estimators. To this extent, let
\mathcal{G}
be a CovNet operator from the unrestricted class
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R}
with kernel
g(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\sum_{s=1}^{R}\lambda_{r,s}\,g_{r}(\text{\bf u})\,g_{s}(\text{\bf v})
. For
\lambda_{N}>0
, define
\mathscr{P}_{\lambda_{N}}\mathcal{G}
to be the CovNet operator obtained by thresholding the eigenvalues of
\Lambda:=((\lambda_{r,s}))
to
\lambda_{N}
. To be precise, if
\Lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{R}\eta_{i}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}
is the eigendecomposition of
\Lambda
, then we define
\Lambda_{\lambda_{N}}=\sum_{i=1}^{R}\min\{\eta_{i},\lambda_{N}\}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}
to be the
\lambda_{N}
-thresholded version of
\Lambda
. We define
\mathscr{P}_{\lambda_{N}}\mathcal{G}
to be the operator with kernel
g_{\lambda_{N}}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\sum_{s=1}^{R}\widetilde{\lambda}_{r,s}\,g_{r}(\text{\bf u})\,g_{s}(\text{\bf v})
, where
\widetilde{\lambda}_{r,s}
is the
(r,s)
-th element of the matrix
\Lambda_{\lambda_{N}}
. By construction,
\mathrm{0}\preceq\Lambda_{\lambda_{N}}\preceq\lambda_{N}\,\mathrm{I}_{R}
and consequently,
\mathscr{P}_{\lambda_{N}}\mathcal{G}
is an element of the restricted class
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R,\lambda_{N}}
. We are now ready to re-define the estimator. Define

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm sh}_{R,N}\in\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm sh}_{R,\sigma}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{N}-\mathcal{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2},\quad\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm d}_{R,L,N}\in\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm d}_{R,L,\sigma}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{N}-\mathcal{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,N}\in\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,\sigma}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{N}-\mathcal{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2},

to be the shallow, the deep and the deepshared CovNet estimators, respectively, but without any restriction on the underlying classes. Now, for a constant
\lambda_{N}>0
, we define our modified estimators as

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm sh}_{R,N}=\mathscr{P}_{\lambda_{N}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm sh}_{R,N},\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm d}_{R,L,N}=\mathscr{P}_{\lambda_{N}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm d}_{R,L,N}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,N}=\mathscr{P}_{\lambda_{N}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,N}.

(6.3)

These modified estimators are consistent, as shown in the following theorem.

Theorem 5
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}\overset{{\rm i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{X}
, where
\mathcal{X}
takes values in
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
with
\mathbb{E}(\|\mathcal{X}\|^{4})<\infty
. Also assume that
\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{X})=0
and
\mathrm{Cov}(\mathcal{X})=\mathcal{C}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{R,N}^{\rm sh}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{R,L,N}^{\rm d}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{R,L,N}^{\rm ds}
be the modified shallow, deep, and deepshared CovNet estimators given by (
6.3
). Assume that
R>d
, and
R\to\infty,\lambda_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
.

(A)

If
dR^{6}\lambda_{N}^{4}\log(R\lambda_{N})/N\to 0
as
N\to\infty
, then the modified shallow CovNet estimator is weakly consistent for
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm sh}_{R,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}\overset{P}{\to}0
. Additionally, if
dR^{6}\lambda_{N}^{4}\log(R\lambda_{N})/N^{1-\delta}\to 0
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, then it is strongly consistent for
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm sh}_{R,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
as
N\to\infty
.

(B)

Let
R>d
. If
L^{4}R^{12}\lambda_{N}^{4}\log^{2}(LR\lambda_{N})/N\to 0
as
N\to\infty
, then the modified deep CovNet estimator is weakly consistent for
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm d}_{R,L,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}\overset{P}{\to}0
. Additionally, if
L^{4}R^{12}\lambda_{N}^{4}\log^{2}(LR\lambda_{N})/N^{1-\delta}\to 0
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, then it is strongly consistent for
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm d}_{R,L,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
as
N\to\infty
.

(C)

Let
R>d
. If
L^{4}R^{10}\lambda_{N}^{4}\log^{2}(LR\lambda_{N})/N\to 0
, then the modified deepshared CovNet estimator is weakly consistent for
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}\overset{P}{\to}0
. Additionally, if
L^{4}R^{10}\lambda_{N}^{4}\log^{2}(LR\lambda_{N})/N^{1-\delta}\to 0
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, then it is strongly consistent for
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm ds}_{R,L,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
as
N\to\infty
.

Remark 10
.

Our derived rates are truly nonparametric, with minimal assumptions on the underlying structure. We assumed
\mathbb{E}(\|\mathcal{X}\|^{4})<\infty
, which is standard for covariance estimation. Moreover, we made no assumption on the underlying covariance operator
\mathcal{C}
. As a consequence, our derived rates are rather slow in terms of the number of parameters of the models. These can be improved by making further assumptions on the random field
\mathcal{X}
or the eigenfunctions of
\mathcal{C}
(e.g., the ones used by
Bauer and Kohler 2019
or
Schmidt-Hieber 2020
in the context of nonparametric regression). However, such specialized treatments are beyond the scope of the present article. If the rank of
\mathcal{C}
is small, which is very often the case in FDA, then a small
R
is enough to control the bias term (see Appendix
B
). On the other hand, such a small
R
gives us a considerable gain in terms of the variance, thus reducing the overall estimation error. However, one should also note that the derived rates are only upper bounds, and we do not claim tightness of the bounds.

6.2
The case of discretely observed fields

The results derived so far are for fully observed random fields. But in practice, we observe the fields on a grid, with possible noise contamination. Here, we develop asymptotic properties of our estimators in this scenario. W.l.o.g., we assume that
\mathcal{Q}=[0,1]^{d}
, and we observe the data on a
K_{1}\times\cdots\times K_{d}
regular grid on
[0,1]^{d}
. To this extent, let
\{T_{1,1}^{K_{1}},\ldots,T_{1,K_{1}}^{K_{1}}\},\ldots,\{T_{d,1}^{K_{d}},\ldots,T_{d,K_{d}}^{K_{d}}\}
be regular partitions of
[0,1]
of sizes
K_{1},\ldots,K_{d}
, respectively. Define
V_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}^{K}=T_{1,i_{1}}^{K_{1}}\times\cdots\times T_{d,i_{d}}^{K_{d}}
to be the
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})
-th
voxel
for
1\leq i_{1}\leq K_{1},\ldots,1\leq i_{d}\leq K_{d}
. The voxels are non-overlapping (i.e.,
V_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}^{K}\cap V_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{d}}^{K}=\emptyset
for
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\neq(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})
), and they form a regular partition of
[0,1]^{d}
. In particular,
|V_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}^{K}|=(\prod_{i=1}^{d}K_{i})^{-1}
. For each random field
\mathcal{X}_{n}
, we make a single measurement at each of the voxels. These measurements are assumed to be of the form

\widetilde{X}_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]=X_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]+E_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}],\qquad 1\leq i_{1}\leq K_{1},\ldots,1\leq i_{d}\leq K_{d},\,n=1,\ldots,N,

(6.4)

where
X^{K}_{n}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]
is a discretization of
\mathcal{X}_{n}
over the
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})
-th voxel and
E_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]
is the corresponding measurement error or noise. We consider two different measurement schemes which relate the discrete object
\mathbf{X}_{n}^{K}=(X_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}])
to the respective field
\mathcal{X}_{n}=(X_{n}(\text{\bf u}):\text{\bf u}\in[0,1]^{d})
.

(M1)

Point-wise measurement:

X_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]=X_{n}(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{d}}),\qquad 1\leq i_{1}\leq K_{1},\ldots,1\leq i_{d}\leq K_{d},

where
(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{d}})\in V_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}^{K}
is a location within the
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})
-th voxel. For the measurements to be meaningful, we need to assume that
\mathcal{X}
has continuous sample paths
(
Hsing and Eubank 2015
, e.g.,)
.

(M2)

Voxel-wise average:

X_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]=\frac{1}{|V_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}^{K}|}\int_{V_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}^{K}}X_{n}(\text{\bf u})\,\mathrm{d}\text{\bf u},\qquad 1\leq i_{1}\leq K_{1},\ldots,1\leq i_{d}\leq K_{d}.

For the measurement errors
E_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]
, we assume that they are i.i.d. with mean
0
and variance
\sigma_{K}^{2}
, and are uncorrelated with the
X_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]
’s. In line with our previous assumptions, we also assume that
\big|E_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]\big|^{2}\leq\beta^{\rm e}_{N,K}
almost surely.

We denote the measurements corresponding to the
n
-th field
\mathcal{X}_{n}
by
\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}_{n}^{K}=(\widetilde{X}_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}])
. Define
\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{N}^{K}=N^{-1}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}_{n}^{K}\otimes\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}_{n}^{K}
to be the empirical covariance based on the discretely observed data. For a generic class of CovNet operators
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R}
, our estimator is given by

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{K}_{R,N}\in\operatornamewithlimits{arg\,min}_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R}}\big\|\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{N}^{K}-\mathbf{G}^{K}\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2},

where
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{F}}
is the Frobenius norm and
\mathbf{G}^{K}
is the discretization of the operator
\mathcal{G}
over the voxels, defined as

\mathbf{G}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d};j_{1},\ldots,j_{d}]=g(v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{d}};v_{j_{1}},\ldots,v_{j_{d}}),\qquad 1\leq i_{1},j_{1}\leq K_{1},\ldots,1\leq i_{d},j_{d}\leq K_{d},

where
g
is the kernel corresponding to
\mathcal{G}
and
(v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{d}})
is a location in the
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})
-th voxel
V_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}^{K}
. If we define
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}^{K}
to be the voxel-wise continuation of
\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{N}^{K}
, with kernel

\widetilde{c}_{N}^{K}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\cdots\sum_{i_{d}=1}^{K_{d}}\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{K_{1}}\cdots\sum_{j_{d}=1}^{K_{d}}\widetilde{C}_{N}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d};j_{1},\ldots,j_{d}]\,\textrm{{1}}\{\text{\bf u}\in V_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}^{K},\text{\bf v}\in V_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{d}}^{K}\},\qquad\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v}\in[0,1]^{d},

then it is easy to see that

\operatornamewithlimits{arg\,min}_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R}}\big\|\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{N}^{K}-\mathbf{G}^{K}\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\approx\operatornamewithlimits{arg\,min}_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}^{K}-\mathcal{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2},

where the approximation holds when the resolution
K_{1}\times\cdots\times K_{d}
is large (see also (
E.28
) in Appendix
E.4
). We will derive theoretical properties for this approximation. In particular, we define

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{{\rm sh},K}_{R,N}\in\operatornamewithlimits{arg\,min}_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R,\lambda_{N}}^{\rm sh}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}^{K}-\mathcal{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2},\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{{\rm d},K}_{R,L,N}\in\operatornamewithlimits{arg\,min}_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R,L,\lambda_{N}}^{\rm d}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}^{K}-\mathcal{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}\text{ and }\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{{\rm ds},K}_{R,L,N}\in\operatornamewithlimits{arg\,min}_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R,L,\lambda_{N}}^{\rm ds}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}^{K}-\mathcal{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2},

(6.5)

to be the shallow, deep and deepshared CovNet estimators based on the discrete measurements. The asymptotic behaviour of these estimators is established in the following theorem.

Theorem 6
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}\overset{{\rm i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{X}
, where
\mathcal{X}
takes values in
\mathcal{L}_{2}([0,1]^{d})
with
\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{X})=0
and
\mathrm{Cov}(\mathcal{X})=\mathcal{C}
. Let the kernel
c
of
\mathcal{C}
is Lipschitz on
[0,1]^{2d}
with Lipschitz constant
\rho
. Consider the measurement model (
6.4
), where the measurement errors
E_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]
are i.i.d. and uncorrelated with
\mathbf{X}_{1}^{K},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{N}^{K}
, and satisfy
\big|E_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]\big|^{2}\leq\beta_{N,K}^{\rm e}
almost surely,
\mathbb{E}(E_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}])=0
,
\textrm{Var}(E_{n}^{K}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}])=\sigma_{K}^{2}
. Suppose that one of the following two hold.

1.

\mathcal{X}
has continuous sample paths,
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq\beta_{N}
almost surely, and the measurements
\mathbf{X}_{1}^{K},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{N}^{K}
are obtained from
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}
via (M1).

2.

\|\mathcal{X}\|^{2}\leq\beta_{N}
almost surely and the measurements
\mathbf{X}_{1}^{K},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{N}^{K}
are obtained from
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}
via (M2).

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{{\rm sh},K}_{R,N}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{{\rm d},K}_{R,L,N}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{{\rm ds},K}_{R,L,N}
be the shallow, deep and deepshared CovNet estimators given by (
6.5
). Then,

\displaystyle{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{{\rm sh},K}_{R,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 18\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R,\lambda_{N}}^{\rm sh}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{dR^{2}\Delta_{N,K}^{4}\log(N)}{N}\bigg)+a_{K},

\displaystyle{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{{\rm d},K}_{R,L,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 18\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R,L,\lambda_{N}}^{\rm d}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{L^{4}R^{8}\Delta_{N,K}^{4}\log^{2}(N)}{N}\bigg)+a_{K},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{{\rm ds},K}_{R,L,N}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 18\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R,L,\lambda_{N}}^{\rm ds}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{L^{4}R^{6}\Delta_{N,K}^{4}\log^{2}(N)}{N}\bigg)+a_{K},

where
\Delta_{N,K}=\max\{2(\beta_{N}+\beta_{N,K}^{\rm e}),R\lambda_{N}\}
and
a_{K}=21\rho^{2}\big(K_{1}^{-2}+\cdots+K_{d}^{-2}\big)+21\sigma_{K}^{4}/(K_{1}\cdots K_{d})
.

The theorem clearly shows the effect of grid size and noise contamination on the estimators. The rates are qualitatively the same as in Theorem
4
, except
\Delta_{N}
is replaced by
\Delta_{N,K}
and a couple of terms depending on
(K_{1},\ldots,K_{d})
are added. The term
\Delta_{N,K}
can be viewed as a noise contaminated version of
\Delta_{N}
. If we assume that
\beta_{N,K}={\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(\beta_{N})
(which can be seen as assuming that the signal-to-noise ratio diverges), then
\Delta_{N,K}
is asymptotically equivalent to
\Delta_{N}
. In this case, we can see a clear separation in the estimation error, one due to the sample size and the other due to the resolution. Among the remaining two terms,
\rho^{2}(K_{1}^{-2}+\cdots+K_{d}^{-2})
arises due to the discretization of the fields, while
\sigma_{K}^{4}/(K_{1}\cdots K_{d})
is due to noise contamination. Thus, for consistency of our estimators, we require
(K_{1}^{-2}+\cdots+K_{d}^{-2})\to 0
, which is ensured if
\min\{K_{1},\ldots,K_{d}\}\to\infty
. Moreover, the noise level
\sigma_{K}
is allowed to diverge, but at a slower rate than
(K_{1}\cdots K_{d})^{1/4}
. The Lipschitz assumption on
c
in the theorem is convenient, but is by no means necessary. It can be verified that consistency of the estimators holds as long as
c
is continuous.

7
Concluding remarks

We have proposed three new classes of neural network models for covariance estimation of functional data observed over multidimensional domains. The advantages of the proposed models include efficient estimation, storage, manipulation and performance guarantees. Our approach is motivated by the demonstrated ability of neural networks in solving complex problems. And indeed, our empirical studies show the superiority of the proposed methods, especially the deepshared CovNet model. At the same time, our methods will also be amenable to the shortcomings of neural networks, e.g., lack of theoretical optimization guarantees for convergence to global minima. But, as is the case with neural networks, despite these limitations, our experimental results appear compelling. Any progress in the study of neural networks will, in principle, translate to a commensurate progress in the understanding of covariance networks.

Throughout the article, we have used the sigmoidal activation function. But, most of the results, especially the ones for the deep CovNet models, can be easily extended to include other activation functions, e.g., the ReLU. In some preliminary numerical studies, we observed similar performance by the sigmoid and the ReLU. We prefer the sigmoid because of the smoothness that it provides, which is often beneficial for functional covariance estimation.

At the level of generality they are derived, our convergence rates are arguably slow. But, these do not reveal the complete picture and are rather a reflection of our completely nonparametric treatment of the problem. These rates can be improved by considering more structured problems, which is now a topic of interest in theoretical studies of neural networks
(
Bauer and Kohler 2019
;
Schmidt-Hieber 2020
)
. Such additional structural assumptions may also allow us to derive approximation errors for the models, which we have not fully addressed here.

Appendices

In these appendices, we give the proofs omitted from the main text, some further mathematical details and additional simulation results. The organization is as follows. In the next section, we provide some mathematical background useful in subsequent developments. In Section
B
, we discuss the bias of the CovNet models – we establish the universal approximation property, and sketch two different ways to derive the rate of convergence of the bias term. In particular, we derive the rate of convergence of the bias for the shallow CovNet model. In Section
C
, we provide some details on the estimation of the CovNet models and also describe a way to estimate the mean function from the data using the CovNet models. In Section
D
, we treat the covering numbers of certain spaces, as these play a fundamental role in the proofs of the asymptotic results. In particular, we derive upper bounds on the covering numbers of the classes of shallow, deep and deepshared CovNet operators. In Section
E
, we provide proofs of the asymptotic results. Finally, in Section
F
, we provide some additional numerical results which were left out in the main text.

A
Mathematical background

We start by summarizing some definitions and background concepts. More details can be found in
Hsing and Eubank 2015
. Let
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a compact set. We denote by
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
the space of all real-valued square-integrable functions on
\mathcal{Q}
. This is a Hilbert space when equipped with the inner product
\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{Q}}f(\text{\bf u})g(\text{\bf u})\,\mathrm{d}\text{\bf u}
for
f,g\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
. A linear map
\mathcal{A}
from
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
onto itself is called bounded if there exists a constant
M\geq 0
such that
\|\mathcal{A}f\|\leq M\|f\|
for all
f\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
, where
\|\cdot\|
is the norm induced by the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, i.e.,
\|f\|=\sqrt{\langle f,f\rangle}
. A bounded linear map is referred to as an
operator
. The minimum value of
M
for which the boundedness condition holds is called the
operator norm
and is denoted by
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\cdot|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{\infty}
. An operator
\mathcal{A}
is
compact
if there exist orthonormal bases (ONBs)
(\psi_{j})_{j\geq 1}
and
(\phi_{j})_{j\geq 1}
of
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
such that
\mathcal{A}f=\sum_{j\geq 1}\lambda_{j}\langle\psi_{j},f\rangle\phi_{j}
. A compact operator
\mathcal{A}
is called
Hilbert-Schmidt
if
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}=\big\{\sum_{j\geq 1}\|\mathcal{A}\psi_{j}\|^{2}\big\}^{1/2}
is finite, where
(\psi_{j})_{j\geq 1}
is a ONB of
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
. As indicated by the notation,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\cdot|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}
does not depend on the particular choice of the basis, and is called the
Hilbert-Schmidt norm
. We will use
\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}))
to denote the class of all Hilbert-Schmidt operators on
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
. An operator
\mathcal{A}
is called positive semi-definite if
\langle\mathcal{A}f,f\rangle\geq 0
for all
f\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
. A compact, positive semi-definite operator is called
trace class
or
nuclear
if
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}=\sum_{j\geq 1}\langle\mathcal{A}\psi_{j},\psi_{j}\rangle
is finite for some ONB
(\psi_{j})_{j\geq 1}
. Again, the sum is independent of the choice of ONB, and
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}
is called the
trace norm
of
\mathcal{A}
. One particular type of operators on
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
, which is of interest to us is the
integral operator
defined as
\mathcal{A}f(\text{\bf u})=\int_{\mathcal{Q}}a(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})\,f(\text{\bf v})\,\mathrm{d}\text{\bf v}
for
\text{\bf u}\in\mathcal{Q},f\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
, where
a\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})
is called the
kernel
of the operator
\mathcal{A}
. An integral operator
\mathcal{A}
is positive semi-definite if and only if the associated kernel
c
is non-negative definite. The operator
\mathcal{A}
and the kernel
a
are linked by an obvious isometry, i.e.,
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}=\|c\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}
.

Now, let
\mathcal{X}=(X(\text{\bf u}):\text{\bf u}\in\mathcal{Q})
be a random element in
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
. For
d=1
,
\mathcal{X}
is usually referred to as a
random curve
, whereas for
d>1
, it is referred to as a
random field
or
random surface
. We assume that
\mathcal{X}
has finite second moment, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}(\|\mathcal{X}\|^{2})<\infty
, which ensures the existence of its mean
m=\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{X})
and covariance
\mathcal{C}=\mathbb{E}\{(\mathcal{X}-m)\otimes(\mathcal{X}-m)\}
of
\mathcal{X}
(both the expectations are understood in the Bochner sense). The mean
m
is an element of
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
. The covariance
\mathcal{C}
is the integral operator associated with the
covariance kernel

c\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})
, where
c(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\mathrm{Cov}(X(\text{\bf u}),X(\text{\bf v}))
. Moreover,
\mathcal{C}
is positive semi-definite and trace class. In this article, we are interested in estimating
\mathcal{C}
based on independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) observations
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}\sim\mathcal{X}
. Because of the isomorphism linking the integral operator and the associated kernel, the problem is equivalent to estimating the kernel
c
.

B
Bias of the CovNet model: universal approximation and rate of convergence

Here, we deal with the bias of the CovNet model. We start by proving that all three CovNet structures can approximate any covariance operator up to arbitrary precision, a.k.a. the
universal approximation
property. These results are instrumental for the consistency of our CovNet estimators. We start with the shallow CovNet structure and give a detailed proof. The proofs for the deep and the deepshared structures are similar, and we discuss those only briefly.

B.1
Universal approximation

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Recall that
c
is the kernel of the covariance operator
\mathcal{C}
. So, by the spectral decomposition of
\mathcal{C}
, we get

c(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\eta_{i}\,\psi_{i}(\text{\bf u})\,\psi_{i}(\text{\bf v}),

(B.1)

where the sum on the right converges in the
\mathcal{L}_{2}
norm on
\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}
. Here,
\eta_{i}
’s are the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{C}
and
\psi_{i}
’s are the corresponding eigenfunctions. Since the covariance operator
\mathcal{C}
is trace-class, we get

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\eta_{i}=\int_{\mathcal{Q}}c(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf u})\,\,\mathrm{d}\text{\bf u}<\infty.

Now, fix
\epsilon>0
and w.l.o.g. let
\epsilon<1
. Since the sum in (
B.1
) converges in the
\mathcal{L}_{2}
norm, we can find an integer
I
(depending on
\epsilon
) such that

\bigg\|c-\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\,\psi_{i}\otimes\,\psi_{i}\bigg\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}<\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

(B.2)

Also, since the functions
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{I}
are in
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
, we can find a positive constant
M
(depending on
\epsilon
) such that
\max_{i=1,\ldots,I}\big\|\psi_{i}\big\|\leq M
. Since
\sigma
is a sigmoidal function, using the density of single hidden layer neural networks in the class of
\mathcal{L}_{2}
functions
(
Györfi et al. 2002
, Theorem 16.2)
, for each
i=1,\ldots,I
, we can find
R_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, coefficients
a_{i,1},\ldots,a_{i,R_{i}}
, weights
\mathbf{w}_{i,1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{i,R_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and biases
b_{i,1},\ldots,b_{i,R_{i}}
such that

\bigg\|\psi_{i}-\sum_{r=1}^{R_{i}}a_{i,r}\,\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{i,r}^{\top}\cdot+\,b_{i,r})\bigg\|<\frac{\epsilon}{{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}(4M+2)}.

(B.3)

Define
\widehat{\psi}_{i}(\text{\bf u})=\sum_{r=1}^{R_{i}}a_{i,r}\,\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{i,r}^{\top}\text{\bf u}+b_{i,r})
for
i=1,\ldots,I
, and

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{R}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\,\widehat{\psi}_{i}(\text{\bf u})\,\widehat{\psi}_{i}(\text{\bf v})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\sum_{r=1}^{R_{i}}\sum_{s=1}^{R_{i}}a_{r,i}\,a_{s,i}\,\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{r,i}^{\top}\text{\bf u}+b_{r,i})\,\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{s,i}^{\top}\text{\bf v}+b_{s,i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\sum_{s=1}^{R}\lambda_{r,s}\,\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{r}^{\top}\text{\bf u}+b_{r})\,\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{s}^{\top}\text{\bf v}+b_{s}),

(B.4)

where
R=\sum_{i=1}^{I}R_{i}
. Here,
\{\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{R}\}
is the collection of all the weights of the
I
neural networks
\widehat{\psi}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\psi}_{I}
, and
\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{R}\}
is the collection of all the biases. The associated matrix
\Lambda=((\lambda_{r,s}))
is block-diagonal with blocks
\Lambda_{i}=\eta_{i}\,(a_{r,i}\,a_{s,i})_{1\leq r,s\leq R_{i}}
. Since
\eta_{i}>0
, each of the
\Lambda_{i}
’s are positive semi-definite, which in turn shows that
\Lambda
is positive semi-definite. Define
c_{I}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\,\psi_{i}(\text{\bf u})\,\psi_{i}(\text{\bf v})
.
Using

\|f\otimes f-g\otimes g\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}\leq 2\|f\|\,\|f-g\|+\|f-g\|^{2},

(B.5)

we get

\displaystyle\|c_{I}-\widehat{c}_{R}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\big\{\psi_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}-\widehat{\psi}_{i}\otimes\widehat{\psi}_{i}\big\}\bigg\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}~~~~\text{(by \eqref{eq:universal_approximation_proof_c_hat_L2})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\big\|\psi_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}-\widehat{\psi}_{i}\otimes\widehat{\psi}_{i}\big\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\,\Bigg\{2M\frac{\epsilon}{{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}(4M+2)}+\bigg(\frac{\epsilon}{{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}(4M+2)}\bigg)^{2}\Bigg\}~~~~\text{(by \eqref{eq:universal_approximation_bound_L2} and \eqref{eq:product_difference_bound_L2})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\frac{\epsilon}{{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}(4M+2)}(2M+1)~~~~~(\text{since }\epsilon<1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon}{2{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|C\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

(B.6)

Finally, combining (
B.2
) and (
B.1
), we get

\displaystyle\|c-\widehat{c}_{R}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}\leq\|c-c_{I}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}+\|c_{I}-\widehat{c}_{R}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}\leq\epsilon,

as intended.

Now, if in addition
c
is continuous, then it admits a similar decomposition as in (
B.1
), where the sum converges absolutely and uniformly
(
Hsing and Eubank 2015
, Theorem 4.6.5)
. So, we can find an integer
I
such that

\bigg\|c-\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\,\psi_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}\bigg\|_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}<\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

Also, the eigenfunctions
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{I}
are now continuous on a compact set
\mathcal{Q}
. So, there exists a positive constant
M
such that
\max_{i=1,\ldots,I}\|\psi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty}(\mathcal{Q})}\leq M
. Since
\sigma
is a sigmoidal function, we can find neural networks
\widehat{\psi}_{i}
of the form (
B.3
) such that

\|\psi_{i}-\widehat{\psi}_{i}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty}(\mathcal{Q})}<\frac{\epsilon}{{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}(4M+2)},

see Lemma 16.1 in
Györfi et al. 2002
. The rest of the proof follows similarly to the previous case upon using a bound similar to (
B.5
) for the
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
norm.
∎

Next, we briefly discuss the universal approximation property of the deep and the deepshared models.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

Recall that the deep CovNet kernel is of the form

c_{\rm d}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\sum_{s=1}^{R}\lambda_{r,s}\,g_{r}(\text{\bf u})\,g_{s}(\text{\bf v}),

where each of the functions
g_{1},\ldots,g_{R}
are individual deep neural networks. To prove the universal approximation property of this structure, we proceed similarly to the case of the shallow CovNet structure. Namely, we decompose
c
as

c(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\,\psi_{i}(\text{\bf u})\,\psi_{i}(\text{\bf v})+\sum_{i=I+1}^{\infty}\eta_{i}\,\psi_{i}(\text{\bf u})\,\psi_{i}(\text{\bf v})=:c_{I}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})+e_{I}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v}),

where
\|c-c_{I}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}\leq\epsilon/2
. Now, for each
i=1,\ldots,I
, we can find deep neural network
\widehat{\psi}_{i}=\sum_{r=1}^{R_{i}}a_{i,r}\,g_{i,r}(\cdot)
of the required form such that
\|\psi_{i}-\widehat{\psi}_{i}\|<\delta
(follows from the universal approximation property of the deep neural network with sigmoid activation, see
Funahashi 1989
). Also, the depth of all these networks can be taken to be the same
(see
Funahashi 1989
, Corollary 1)
. Now, by defining
\widehat{c}_{R}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\,\widehat{\psi}_{i}(\text{\bf u})\,\widehat{\psi}_{i}(\text{\bf v})
, it is easy to verify that
\widehat{c}_{R}
has the deep CovNet structure and approximates
c
up to the desired precision.

Next, consider the deepshared structure. Observe that for any deep CovNet kernel with depth
L
and number of nodes
R
, we can find a deepshared CovNet kernel with depth
L
and number of nodes
R^{\prime}
, such that the two structures are the same (by considering a wider network and deleting some of the connections, see Figures
2
and
3
). Thus, for a fixed depth, the complexity of the deep and the deepshared structures is the same (when we allow the number of nodes to vary). Thus, the result for the deepshared CovNet model follows from the universal approximation of the deep CovNet model.
∎

Remark 11
.

The results that we have proved establish the universal approximation property of the three CovNet models without any restriction on the parameters. But, to establish consistency of the estimators and their rates of convergence (Section
6
), we need to impose restrictions on the eigenstructure of the matrix
\Lambda
. Therefore, we need to control the bias for this restricted class of operators. The universal approximation property for the restricted class follows easily. By the universal approximation property of the unrestricted class, for a given
\mathcal{C}
and
\epsilon>0
, we can find a CovNet operator
\mathcal{G}
(of any of the three types) such that
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{C}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}<\epsilon
. Now, let
\Lambda
be the matrix associated with
\mathcal{G}
. Since
\Lambda
is a positive semi-definite matrix, we can find
\lambda_{0}>0
such that
\Lambda\preceq\lambda_{0}\,\mathrm{I}
(e.g., by takings
\lambda_{0}
to be the largest eigenvalue of
\Lambda
). This shows that for any
\mathcal{C}
, we can find a CovNet operator from the restricted class that can approximate
\mathcal{C}
up to arbitrary precision, thus establishing the universal approximation property with the additional condition.

B.2
Rate of convergence of the bias term

Here, we will derive the rate of convergence of the bias for the (possibly restricted) class of CovNet operators. We will describe two possible ways to obtain the rates – (a) by imposing conditions on the eigenstructure of
\mathcal{C}
or (b) by imposing conditions on the observation
\mathcal{X}
.

B.2.1
Restrictions on the covariance

By the eigendecomposition, we can write
\mathcal{C}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\eta_{i}\,\psi_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}
, where
(\eta_{i})_{i\geq 1}
is the sequence of non-increasing eigenvalues of
\mathcal{C}
and
(\psi_{i})_{i\geq 1}
is the corresponding sequence of eigenfunctions. For
I\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\mathcal{C}_{I}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\,\psi_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}
to be the truncated version of
\mathcal{C}
. Then,

{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}_{I}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i>I}\eta_{i}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(a_{I}),

where
a_{I}
depends on the
eigen-decay
of
\mathcal{C}
. Now, for each
i=1,\ldots,I
, suppose that we can find (shallow/deep/deepshared) neural networks
\widehat{\psi}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\psi}_{K}
such that

\|\psi_{i}-\widehat{\psi}_{i}\|=\mathcal{O}(b_{L_{i},R_{i}}),\qquad i=1,\ldots,I,

where
L_{i},R_{i}
are the parameters (depth and/or width) of
\widehat{\psi}_{i}
. The rate of the approximation error
b_{L_{i},R_{i}}
depends on additional structural assumptions on the eigenfunctions
(
Mhaskar 1996
;
Bauer and Kohler 2019
;
Ohn and Kim 2019
;
Schmidt-Hieber 2020
;
Langer 2021
, e.g.,)
, which can be imposed by means of additional structural assumptions on the kernel
c
. Now, if we define
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\eta_{i}\,\widehat{\psi}_{i}\otimes\widehat{\psi}_{i}
, then it is easy to see that
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}
is a CovNet operator with
R
nodes (and possibly of depth
L
) such that

{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{C}-\widehat{\mathcal{C}}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}\leq 2\Big\{{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}_{I}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{C}_{I}-\widehat{\mathcal{C}}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}\Big\}=\mathcal{O}(a_{I})+\mathcal{O}(b_{I,L,R}),

where
b_{I,L,R}
can be obtained from the
b_{L_{i},R_{i}}
’s using (
B.5
). Here, the parameters
R
and
L
of the CovNet operator depend on
I,L_{i},R_{i}
. Finally, the exact rate of convergence of the bias term can be obtained by carefully scrutinizing the terms
a_{I}
and
b_{I,L,R}
as functions of
I,L,R
, and choosing
I
appropriately.

B.2.2
Restrictions on the observations

The idea here is similar to the one used before. Instead of the eigendecomposition, we will make use of a different type of approximation result. The following lemma will be instrumental in our derivation, which is a suitable adaptation of Lemma 16.7 in
Györfi et al. 2002
. The proof follows easily from the proof of Lemma 16.7 in
Györfi et al. 2002
, so we omit it.

Lemma 1
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be an index set, and
\{\phi_{t}:t\in\mathcal{T}\}
be a collection of real-valued functions on a compact domain
\mathcal{Q}
such that
\|\phi_{t}\|\leq B
for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
. Let
f:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function such that there exists a probability measure
b
on
\mathcal{T}
satisfying

f(\text{\bf u})=\int_{\mathcal{Q}}\phi_{t}(\text{\bf u})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(t)\quad\forall\,\text{\bf u}\in\mathcal{Q}.

Then, for every
I\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a function
f_{I}(\text{\bf u})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}w_{i}\phi_{t_{i}}(\text{\bf u})
such that

\|f-f_{I}\|\leq\frac{B}{\sqrt{I}}.

Moreover, the coefficients
w_{i}
are non-negative and
\sum_{i=1}^{I}w_{i}=1
.

Now, suppose that the random field
\mathcal{X}
satisfies
\mathbb{P}(\|\mathcal{X}\|^{2}\leq\beta)=1
. Recall that the covariance kernel
c
of
\mathcal{X}=(X(\text{\bf u}):\text{\bf u}\in\mathcal{Q})
is defined as

c(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\mathrm{Cov}(X(\text{\bf u}),X(\text{\bf v}))=\mathbb{E}(X(\text{\bf u})X(\text{\bf v}))=\int_{\Omega}X(\text{\bf u},\omega)X(\text{\bf v},\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega),\quad\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v}\in\mathcal{Q},

for some set
\Omega
and the probability measure
\mathbb{P}
on
\Omega
. Thus, by defining
\mathcal{T}=\Omega
and
\phi_{\omega}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=X(\text{\bf u},\omega)X(\text{\bf v},\omega)
for
\omega\in\Omega
, we see that

\|\phi_{\omega}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}^{2}=\iint_{\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}}X^{2}(\text{\bf u},\omega)X^{2}(\text{\bf v},\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\text{\bf u}\,\mathrm{d}\text{\bf v}=\|\mathcal{X}(\omega)\|^{4}\leq\beta^{2}~~\text{almost surely}.

Also,
c(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\int_{\Omega}\phi_{\omega}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)
. So, using Lemma
1
, for every
I\in\mathbb{N}
, we can find
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{I}\in\Omega
and non-negative constants
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{I}
such that, defining
c_{I}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\,\phi_{\omega_{i}}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})
, we get

{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}_{I}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}=\|c-c_{I}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}^{2}=\iint_{\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}}\{c(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})-c_{I}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})\}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\text{\bf u}\,\mathrm{d}\text{\bf v}\leq\frac{\beta^{2}}{I}=\mathcal{O}(I^{-1}),

(B.7)

where
\mathcal{C}_{I}
is the integral operator associated with the kernel
c_{I}
. Observe that

c_{I}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\phi_{\omega_{i}}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\,X_{i}(\text{\bf u})\,X_{i}(\text{\bf v}),\quad\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v}\in\mathcal{Q},

where the functions
\mathcal{X}_{i}:=\mathcal{X}(\omega_{i})
. Thus, proceeding as in the previous section, we can obtain (a bound on) the rate of convergence of the bias of the CovNet operator. The exact rate, in this case, will depend on additional structural assumptions on the functions
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{I}
, or equivalently on
\mathcal{X}
.

We demonstrate this by deriving the rate for the restricted shallow CovNet operator. Suppose that
\mathcal{X}
takes values in
\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{Q})
, the
Sobolev space
of order
\alpha
in
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})
. Further, let
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{Q})}^{2}\leq\beta
almost surely (if
\alpha\geq 2
, this also implies that
\|\mathcal{X}\|^{2}\leq\beta
almost surely). Thus, for every
i=1,\ldots,I
,
\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{Q})
. By Theorem 2.1 in
Mhaskar 1996
, for every
R\in\mathbb{N}
, we can find weights
\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{R}
, bias
b
, and continuous functionals
f_{1},\ldots,f_{R}
on
\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{Q})
such that, defining
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(\text{\bf u})=\sum_{r=1}^{R}f_{r}(\mathcal{X}_{i})\,\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{r}^{\top}\text{\bf u}+b)
, we get

\|\mathcal{X}_{i}-\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\|\lesssim R^{-\alpha/d}\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\|_{\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{Q})}\lesssim R^{-\alpha/d}.

Since the functionals
f_{1},\ldots,f_{R}
are continuous, there exist finite constants
a_{1},\ldots,a_{R}
such that

|f_{r}(\mathcal{X}_{i})|\leq a_{r}\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\|_{\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{Q})}\leq a_{r}\sqrt{\beta},\quad r=1,\ldots,R.

Thus, by defining
\widetilde{c}_{I,R}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\,\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(\text{\bf u})\,\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(\text{\bf v})
, we get

\displaystyle\big\|c_{I}-\widetilde{c}_{I,R}\big\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\Big\|\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{i}-\mathcal{X}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{i}\Big\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\Big\{\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{i}-\mathcal{X}_{i}\big\|^{2}+2\big\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\big\|\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{i}-\mathcal{X}_{i}\big\|\Big\}~~~~\text{(using \eqref{eq:product_difference_bound_L2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim R^{-2\alpha/d}+R^{-\alpha/d}\asymp R^{-\alpha/d}.

(B.8)

Here, we have used that
\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}=1
, and
\alpha\geq 1,R\geq 1
implies
R^{-2\alpha/d}={\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}\big(R^{-\alpha/d}\big)
. Now, combining (
B.7
) and (
B.2.2
), we get

\displaystyle\big\|c-\widetilde{c}_{I,R}\big\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}\leq\big\|c-c_{I}\big\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}+\big\|c_{I}-\widetilde{c}_{I,R}\big\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}\lesssim I^{-1/2}+R^{-\alpha/d}.

By choosing
I\asymp R^{2\alpha/d}
, we get that

\big\|c-\widetilde{c}_{I,R}\big\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}\lesssim R^{-\alpha/d}.

(B.9)

Now, observe that

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{I,R}(\text{\bf u},\text{\bf v})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\,\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(\text{\bf u})\,\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(\text{\bf v})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\sum_{r=1}^{R}\sum_{s=1}^{R}\beta_{i,r}\,\beta_{i,s}\,\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{r}^{\top}\text{\bf u}+b)\,\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{s}^{\top}\text{\bf v}+b)~~(\text{where }\beta_{i,r}=f_{r}(\mathcal{X}_{i}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\sum_{s=1}^{R}\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\,\beta_{i,r}\,\beta_{i,s}\Bigg)\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{r}^{\top}\text{\bf u}+b)\,\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{s}^{\top}\text{\bf v}+b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\sum_{s=1}^{R}\lambda_{r,s}\,\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{r}^{\top}\text{\bf u}+b)\,\sigma(\mathbf{w}_{s}^{\top}\text{\bf v}+b).

(B.10)

Thus,
\widetilde{c}_{I,R}
is a shallow CovNet kernel. Since
\gamma_{i}\geq 0
for each
i
, the matrix
\Lambda=(\lambda_{r,s})
is positive semi-definite. Also,
\Lambda\preceq\lambda_{R}{\rm I}_{R}
, where
\lambda_{R}=\beta\sum_{r=1}^{R}a_{r}^{2}
. This follows from the following facts.

(i)

For a matrix
A=\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times R}
,
\text{\bf x}^{\top}A\text{\bf x}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}^{\top}\text{\bf x})^{2}\leq\|\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}\|^{2}\|\text{\bf x}\|^{2}
for every
x
, implying that
A\preceq\|\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}\|^{2}{\rm I}_{R}
.

(ii)

For a collection of matrices
(A_{i})_{i=1}^{I}
and
(B_{i})_{i=1}^{I}
satisfying
0\preceq A_{i}\preceq B_{i}
, and non-negative scalars
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{I}
,
\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}A_{i}\preceq\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}B_{i}
.

(iii)

By (i) and (ii), for non-negative scalars
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{I}
and vectors
\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}_{1},\ldots,\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}_{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{R}
,
\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}_{i}\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}_{i}^{\top}\preceq\big(\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}_{i}\|^{2}\big)\,{\rm I}_{R}
.

(iv)

\Lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}_{i}\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}_{i}^{\top}
, where
\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}_{i}=(\beta_{i,1},\ldots,\beta_{i,R})^{\top}
. For each
i=1,\ldots,I
,
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}_{i}\|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{R}\beta_{i,r}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{R}f_{r}^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{i})\leq\beta\sum_{r=1}^{R}a_{r}^{2}
. Also,
\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}=1
implies that
\sum_{i=1}^{I}\gamma_{i}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}_{i}\|^{2}\leq\beta\sum_{r=1}^{R}a_{r}^{2}
.

Using this along with (
B.9
) and (
B.2.2
), it follows that if we choose
\lambda_{N}=\beta\sum_{r=1}^{R}a_{r}^{2}
, then

\inf_{\mathcal{G}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R,\lambda_{N}}}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{G}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\lesssim R^{-\alpha/d}.

(B.11)

C
Further details on the implementation of the CovNet models

In this section, we present further details on the implementation of the CovNet models. We start by proving Proposition
1
, which justifies the use of the alternative formulation for estimating the CovNet models.

Proof of Proposition
1
.

By construction, it is clear that
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm NN}_{R,N}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R}
for every
N\geq 1
. So, we will only show that for
N>R
, the other inclusion holds, i.e.,
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{R,N}^{\rm NN}
. This amounts to showing that for every positive semi-definite matrix
\Lambda=(\lambda_{r,s})\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times R}
, we can find
\xi_{n,r}
for
n=1,\ldots,N
,
r=1,\ldots,R
, such that
\lambda_{r,s}=N^{-1}\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\xi_{n,r}-\bar{\xi}_{r})(\xi_{n,s}-\bar{\xi}_{s})
.

For
N>R
, we can always find
N
vectors
\bm{\zeta}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\zeta}_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{R}
such that the rank of the empirical covariance based of these vectors is
R
. For instance, one can take
\bm{\zeta}_{i}=\mathbf{e}_{i}
, the
i
-th canonical vector in
\mathbb{R}^{R}
for
i=1,\ldots,R
,
\bm{\zeta}_{R+1}=-\textrm{{1}}
, and
\bm{\zeta}_{i}=\mathbf{0}
for
i=R+2,\ldots,N
, to check that the corresponding empirical covariance matrix is of the form
N^{-1}(\mathrm{I}_{R}+\textrm{{1}}\textrm{{1}}^{\top})
, which is of rank
R
. Let us denote this empirical covariance by
\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}
. Thus,
\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}
is of full rank, and hence positive definite. So, we can find a positive definite matrix
\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}^{-1/2}
, so that
\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}^{-1/2}\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}^{-1/2}=\mathrm{I}_{R}
. Again, since
\Lambda=(\Lambda_{r,s})
is positive semi-definite, we can find a positive semi-definite matrix
\Lambda^{1/2}
such that
\Lambda^{1/2}\Lambda^{1/2}=\Lambda
. Define,
\bm{\xi}_{n}=\Lambda^{1/2}\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}^{-1/2}\bm{\zeta}_{n}
for
n=1,\ldots,N
. Then, it is easy to verify that the empirical covariance of
\bm{\xi}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\xi}_{N}
is
\Lambda
. Now, let
\xi_{n,r}
be the
r
-th component of
\bm{\xi}_{n}
. For
n=1,\ldots,N
, define
\mathcal{X}^{\rm NN}_{n}(\text{\bf u})=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\xi_{n,r}\,g_{r}(\text{\bf u})
. It can be easily verified that the empirical covariance of
\mathcal{X}_{1}^{\rm NN},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}^{\rm NN}
is the operator with kernel
\sum_{r=1}^{R}\sum_{s=1}^{R}\lambda_{r,s}\,g_{r}(\text{\bf u})\,g_{s}(\text{\bf v})
. This establishes the other inclusion.
∎

Next, we give a detailed derivation of the equivalence between the original loss functions, and the loss functions expressed in terms of the observations
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}
and the fitted networks
\mathcal{X}_{1}^{\rm NN},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}^{\rm NN}
.

C.1
Detailed derivation of the loss function

Let
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}
be the observed fields and
\mathcal{X}_{1}^{\rm NN},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}^{\rm NN}
be the fitted networks:

\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}(\text{\bf u})=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\xi_{n,r}\,g_{r}(\text{\bf u}),\qquad n=1,\ldots,N.

To begin with, we assume that all the fields are centered, so that
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{X}_{n}=0=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}
. The loss function is defined as

\displaystyle\ell:=\ell(\Theta)={\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{N}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2},

where
\Theta
denotes all the
learnable parameters
of the model. Now,
\ell
can be written as

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle={\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{N}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}={\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\mathcal{X}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}-\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}{\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\mathcal{X}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}-\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN},\mathcal{X}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{m}-\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle}_{2},

(C.1)

where
{\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle}_{2}
is the Hilbert-Schmidt inner-product. Now, the inner product in the last step equals

\displaystyle{\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\mathcal{X}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{m}\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle}_{2}+{\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN},\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle}_{2}

\displaystyle\kern 172.22168pt-{\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\mathcal{X}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle}_{2}-{\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN},\mathcal{X}_{m}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{m}\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle}_{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{m}\rangle^{2}+\langle\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN},\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\rangle^{2}-\langle\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\rangle^{2}-\langle\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN},\mathcal{X}_{m}\rangle^{2}.

Here, we have used that
{\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\kern-2.15277pt\left\langle\mathcal{X}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{2}\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle\kern-2.15277pt\right\rangle}_{2}=\langle\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{1}\rangle\,\langle\mathcal{X}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{2}\rangle
. Plugging this back into (
C.1
), we get that

\ell=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\langle\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{m}\rangle^{2}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\langle\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN},\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\rangle^{2}-\frac{2}{N^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\langle\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\rangle^{2}.

Thus, the loss
\ell
can be obtained from the inner products
\langle\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{m}\rangle
,
\langle\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN},\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\rangle
, without forming the high-order objects
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{N}
or
\mathcal{X}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}
. When the fields are not centered, one can first center them by subtracting the mean and work with the centered fields. Any mean estimation method can be used for this purpose. We can also use a different approach, which allows us to simultaneously estimate the mean.

C.2
Simultaneous estimation of the mean

In the previous derivations, we assumed that the fields
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}
as well as the fitted fields
\mathcal{X}_{1}^{\rm NN},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{N}^{\rm NN}
are centered, i.e.,
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}=\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}^{\rm NN}=0
. When this is not the case, the loss function can be written as

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle={\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{X}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}-\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}+\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}^{\rm NN}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}^{\rm NN}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\Bigg\{{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{X}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}-\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}^{\rm NN}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}^{\rm NN}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=2\,\big\{\widetilde{\ell}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}-\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}^{\rm NN}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}^{\rm NN}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\big\},

where
\widetilde{\ell}
is the loss function without centering. In this case, instead of minimizing the loss functions
\ell
, one can minimize

\widetilde{\ell}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}-\bar{\mathcal{X}}^{\rm NN}_{N}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{X}}^{\rm NN}_{N}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}.

(C.2)

Of course, this is an upper bound to the actual criterion. But, in practice, this gives a reasonable approximation and produces reasonable results. As already demonstrated,
\widetilde{\ell}
can be computed efficiently, without forming the high-order objects. Using similar techniques, it can be shown that

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}-\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}^{\rm NN}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{N}^{\rm NN}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\langle\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{m}\rangle\bigg)^{2}+\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\langle\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\rm NN},\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\rangle\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\kern 172.22168pt-2\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\langle\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\rm NN}\rangle\bigg)^{2},

which again depends on the inner products, thus allowing for efficient computation of the criterion (
C.2
). Moreover, as a by-product, we get an estimate of the mean as follows. As before, we minimize the criterion w.r.t. the parameters of
g_{1},\ldots,g_{R}
, and the new parameters
\xi_{n,r}
, to get estimated fields

\mathcal{X}^{\rm NN}_{n}(\text{\bf u})=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\hat{\xi}_{n,r}\,\widehat{g}_{r}(\text{\bf u}),\qquad n=1,\ldots,N.

The mean field is then obtained as the empirical mean based on the fitted fields, i.e.,

\widehat{m}(\text{\bf u})=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\bar{\hat{\xi}}_{r}\,\widehat{g}_{r}(\text{\bf u}),\qquad\text{\bf u}\in\mathcal{Q},

where
\bar{\hat{\xi}}_{r}=N^{-1}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\hat{\xi}_{n,r}
. Thus, once we estimate the parameters of the model, we can get an estimate of the mean with negligible computational overhead.

(a) Ex 1 with
I=5

(b) Ex 1 with
I=10

(c) Ex 1 with
I=20

Fig 11:
Estimated mean surfaces by different CovNet models in the Fourier basis example with different choices of
I
. The empirical mean surfaces are also shown. The relative estimation errors (in %) are shown at the bottom of every figure. The plots are heatmaps of the 2D mean surfaces.

We demonstrate the usefulness of this method by means of a simulation study. We generated
500
observations from the Gaussian process on
[0,1]^{2}
with mean surface
m
and covariance kernel
c
on a regular grid of resolution
25\times 25
. We took
c
to be the Matérn covariance kernel (Ex 5 in Section
5
) with
\nu=0.01
. For the mean function, we considered two different setups:

Ex 1

Fourier basis
:
m(s,t)=1+\sum_{i=1}^{I}(-1)^{i}i^{-2}\phi_{i}(s)\,\phi_{i}(t)
, where
\phi_{i}(t)=\sqrt{2}\cos(i\pi t)
for
i\geq 1
.

Ex 2

Legendre basis
:
m(s,t)=1+\sum_{i=1}^{I}(-1)^{i}(2i+1)\phi_{i}(s-0.5)\,\phi_{i}(t-0.5)
, where
\phi_{i}
is the Legendre polynomial of degree
i
.

In both the cases, the number of components
I
controls the complexity of the mean. The estimated mean surfaces using different CovNet models, along with the true mean and the empirical mean, for different choices of
I
are shown in Figures
11
and
12
. For the CovNet models, we used cross-validation to select the hyperparameters. We also calculated the estimation error
\|m-\widehat{m}\|/\|m\|
for all the estimators using Monte-Carlo integration, which are shown in the figures. These results clearly exhibit the usefulness of the proposed mean estimation technique. The estimated mean surfaces using the shallow and the deepshared models are very close to the truth. The advantage of having a purely functional form over the discretized empirical estimator is also visible in the figures and is reflected in the estimation errors. The results for the deep CovNet model are, however, not very promising. The deep CovNet model is not able to capture the truth at all. This is perhaps due to the complexity of the deep model, which suggests the need to regularize the deep CovNet model. Further evidence on the need for regularization is furnished by the deepshared model, which has the best performance in all the scenarios. In particular, the deepshared model is able to capture minute details which the shallow model has missed (see Figures
11
(b),
11
(c),
12
(c)).

One can notice that in (
C.2
), the criterion is composed of two components, one accounting for the covariance, while the other accounting for the mean. Instead of simply adding these two components, one may consider a weighted average of the two, depending on their importance. However, we will not pursue this idea further in this article.

I=5
